Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Auth.js server-configuration error reaching production (fail-open precondition) AIExploitation High
Hunts application/stdout logs for the specific Auth.js v5 configuration-error strings that flip the `auth()` object from null to a truthy error object, causing `!!auth` checks to fail open for every request. The article itself recommends treating any `[auth][error]` line as a failed health check, so this is the root-cause detection. Rationale: Uses the exact Auth.js error identifiers named in the advisory (`[auth][error]`, `InvalidEndpoints`, `MissingSecret`) plus the user-facing server-config error string; all four confirmed as real Auth.js error classes via authjs.dev/reference/core/errors and upstream issue #11934. These strings only emit when the config is broken, i.e. exactly when the fail-open condition is active. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/nextauthjs/next-auth/issues/11934 • https://authjs.dev/reference/core/errors • https://cvereports.com/reports/GHSA-8FPG-XM3F-6CX3
ATT&CKT1556
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Dev/staging/preview deployments intentionally running with incomplete provider config emit [auth][error] routinely
  • CI builds surfacing MissingSecret during Next.js build since 5.0.0-beta.16 (upstream issue #10538)
  • A newly added OAuth provider misconfigured on a non-production branch preview
  • Log aggregation of test/debug output that quotes the error string verbatim
  • Deliberate DR/chaos testing of auth failure modes
Suppression filter
| where Computer !has "dev" and Computer !has "staging" and Computer !has "preview" and Computer !has "-ci"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the emitting host is production (not dev/preview). If prod, the app is currently fail-open — every visitor is treated as authenticated. Check the timestamp: has this host served traffic since the first error line? 2) CONTAIN: Roll back the offending deploy or restore the missing env var (AUTH_SECRET / provider issuer). If rollback is slow, take the affected instance out of the load balancer. 3) HUNT: Pull all successful requests to protected routes on that host since FirstSeen (see the fail-open exploitation UC) to scope who accessed what while auth was broken; review for sensitive-data or privileged-action access.
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where SyslogMessage has "[auth][error]"
    or SyslogMessage has "InvalidEndpoints"
    or SyslogMessage has "MissingSecret"
    or SyslogMessage has "There was a problem with the server configuration"
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), Count=count(), SampleMessage=any(SyslogMessage) by Computer, ProcessName
| order by FirstSeen desc
title: Auth.js Server-Configuration Error Reaching Production (Fail-Open Precondition)
id: b3f1c2a4-7d9e-4a1b-8c2d-1f4e6a9b0c31
description: Detects Auth.js (next-auth v5) server-configuration error strings in application logs. These errors populate the auth() object with a truthy error, causing existence-based auth checks (!!auth) to fail open (GHSA-8fpg-xm3f-6cx3).
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-8fpg-xm3f-6cx3
  - https://authjs.dev/reference/core/errors
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/27
tags:
  - attack.t1556
  - attack.defense_evasion
logsource:
  category: application
  product: nodejs
detection:
  selection:
    message|contains:
      - '[auth][error]'
      - 'InvalidEndpoints'
      - 'MissingSecret'
      - 'There was a problem with the server configuration'
  condition: selection
level: high
source:nodejs ("[auth][error]" OR InvalidEndpoints OR MissingSecret OR "There was a problem with the server configuration")
# log group: /aws/lambda/*  (Next.js SSR / API-route function stdout)
fields @timestamp, @logStream, @message
| filter @message like /\[auth\]\[error\]/ or @message like /InvalidEndpoints/ or @message like /MissingSecret/ or @message like /There was a problem with the server configuration/
| sort @timestamp desc
CLOUD
index=* ("[auth][error]" OR "InvalidEndpoints" OR "MissingSecret" OR "There was a problem with the server configuration")
| stats earliest(_time) as firstSeen latest(_time) as lastSeen count values(source) as sources by host
| convert ctime(firstSeen) ctime(lastSeen)
| sort - lastSeen
Auth.js fail-open exploited: protected routes served 200/302 during an active auth-error window AIExploitation Medium
Correlates Auth.js `[auth][error]` log lines with successful (200/302) HTTP responses on the same host inside a short window — the observable signature of the app granting access while the auth layer is broken. This catches the vulnerability being exploited (unauthenticated visitors served protected content), not just the misconfiguration. Rationale: Ties the article's exact fail-open string (`[auth][error]`) to served-content status codes (200/302) on the same host within 10 minutes — the mechanism the advisory describes (`!!auth` true for every request while broken). Correlation on host + time isolates the exploitation from the mere presence of the error. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/nextauthjs/next-auth/issues/11934 • https://cvereports.com/reports/GHSA-8FPG-XM3F-6CX3
ATT&CKT1190 T1556
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimately authenticated users receiving 200/302 during the same window as a transient auth error
  • Uptime/health-check probes hitting genuinely public routes returning 200
  • Static asset requests (js/css/img) returning 200 unrelated to auth-gated routes
  • A single transient OAuth provider timeout logged as [auth][error] while auth otherwise functions normally
Suppression filter
| where csUriStem !endswith ".js" and csUriStem !endswith ".css" and csUriStem !endswith ".ico" and csUriStem !endswith ".png" and csUriStem !endswith ".svg" and csUriStem !endswith ".woff2"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the 200/302 responses are to auth-protected routes (not public pages or static assets). Correlate cIP against known-authenticated sessions — unrecognised IPs receiving protected content during the error window are the strongest signal. 2) CONTAIN: If protected content was served to unauthenticated callers, roll back/fix the config (restore AUTH_SECRET or provider issuer) and pull the host from rotation; invalidate any sessions issued during the window. 3) HUNT: Enumerate every distinct src IP served 200 during all error windows on this host; check downstream logs for data export, privileged mutations, or account-takeover actions performed under the fail-open.
let ErrWindow = 10m;
let AuthErrors = Syslog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
    | where SyslogMessage has "[auth][error]"
    | project ErrTime = TimeGenerated, Computer, AuthError = SyslogMessage;
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where scStatus in (200, 302)
| where csMethod in ("GET","POST")
| join kind=inner AuthErrors on Computer
| where TimeGenerated between (ErrTime .. ErrTime + ErrWindow)
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, scStatus, ErrTime, AuthError
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| join type=inner dest [ search index=* ("[auth][error]" OR "MissingSecret" OR "InvalidEndpoints") | stats count by host | rename host as dest | fields dest ]

Auth.js config break on deploy: first-ever [auth][error] on a previously-healthy host AIExploitation High
Baseline anti-join that surfaces the exact moment a previously-working deployment becomes misconfigured — the article's named trigger ('an environment variable is changed or removed during a deploy'). Flags hosts emitting `[auth][error]` for the first time versus a 14-day baseline, catching the silent transition from fail-closed to fail-open. Rationale: Directly targets the advisory's stated risk narrative — a deploy removing/altering an env var flips a healthy app to fail-open. The 14-day baseline anti-join on `[auth][error]` isolates the first-time-broken transition, which is far higher-fidelity for prod than any-occurrence matching (dev noise typically pre-exists in the baseline). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-8fpg-xm3f-6cx3 • https://github.com/nextauthjs/next-auth/issues/11934
ATT&CKT1556
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • First deployment of a brand-new app/host with no prior baseline (expected initial errors)
  • Host re-imaged or renamed so it appears 'new' though auth was already broken
  • Blue/green or autoscale instance coming online mid-rollout with a transient config gap
  • Baseline window shorter than an intermittent provider's error cadence, so a rare blip reads as first-seen
Suppression filter
| where Computer !has "dev" and Computer !has "staging" and Computer !has "preview" and Computer !has "-ci"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm this host had a healthy 14-day baseline and just started erroring — check whether a deploy/config change landed immediately before FirstSeen (CI/CD timeline, env-var change history). 2) CONTAIN: Treat as an active fail-open on a production host: roll back the deploy or restore the missing config value, and remove the instance from the load balancer until auth() returns null-on-failure again. 3) HUNT: Pivot to the fail-open exploitation UC for this host/window to enumerate what was accessed unauthenticated between FirstSeen and remediation; verify no privileged actions or data reads occurred.
let Baseline = Syslog
    | where TimeGenerated between (ago(14d) .. ago(1d))
    | where SyslogMessage has "[auth][error]"
    | summarize by Computer;
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where SyslogMessage has "[auth][error]"
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on Computer
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), Count=count(), SampleMessage=any(SyslogMessage) by Computer, ProcessName
| order by FirstSeen desc
APPLICATION
index=* "[auth][error]" earliest=-1d
| stats min(_time) as firstSeen count values(source) as sources by host
| where firstSeen >= relative_time(now(), "-1d")
| join type=leftanti host [ search index=* "[auth][error]" earliest=-14d latest=-1d | stats count by host | fields host ]
| convert ctime(firstSeen)
| sort - firstSeen

CL-STA-1114 (Void Blizzard) Zimbra espionage C2/exfil infrastructure contact AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts any host resolving or connecting to the nine hard-coded C2 domains or nine C2 IPs used by CL-STA-1114 / Void Blizzard to receive stolen Zimbra webmail data (credentials, 2FA scratch codes, CSRF tokens, 90-day mail archive). Article-specific because it matches the exact IOC set Unit 42 attributes to this campaign, not a generic beacon shape. Rationale: Uses the exact nine C2 domains and nine C2 IPs Unit 42 attributes to CL-STA-1114; cross-checked against CISA AA26-204A and The Hacker News which independently confirm the Void Blizzard/LAUNDRY BEAR Zimbra campaign, so the IOCs meet the two-source bar. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa26-204a • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/russian-espionage-group-exploited.html • https://cyberscoop.com/russian-laundry-bear-zimbra-exploit/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel platform or sandbox detonating the same IOCs from a research host
  • Security scanner / EDR retro-hunt actively probing the IOC list
  • DNS sinkhole or protective-DNS appliance logging the resolution before blocking
  • A legitimately-registered look-alike domain reclaimed post-takedown resolving to benign parking infrastructure
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","SenseIR.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the host and the initiating process (browser vs. service); confirm the user has an active Zimbra webmail session and whether the org runs unpatched ZCS (<10.0.18 / <10.1.13). 2) Contain: block all nine domains/IPs at proxy+DNS, isolate the host, and force-reset the affected user's Zimbra password and 2FA scratch codes (the payload steals both). 3) Hunt: pivot on the user/device for the last 90 days of mailbox access, search all endpoints for any of the nine IOCs, and review Zimbra logs for the CSS @import XSS mail that triggered it.

Inbound HTML-attachment lure exploiting Zimbra XSS (CVE-2025-66376) AIDelivery Medium
Hunts inbound emails delivered with an HTML/HTM attachment (or news-headline lure body) to webmail users — the CL-STA-1114 delivery vector that carries the obfuscated Base64/SVG payload abusing Zimbra's CSS @import XSS. Article-specific in that it targets the zero-click HTML-borne exploit rather than generic malicious attachments. Rationale: Article states initial access is a phishing email with an HTML attachment or embedded HTML carrying the Base64/SVG payload; corroborating CISA/SentinelOne detail confirms CVE-2025-66376 fires on viewing crafted HTML mail, so inbound HTML attachments to webmail users are the delivery pivot. Kept as hunting given benign HTML attachments are common. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2025-66376/ • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa26-204a
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1203
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Marketing/newsletter platforms that send rich HTML as an attachment
  • Automated report/invoice systems delivering .htm exports
  • Internal ticketing or monitoring tools emailing HTML dashboards inbound via a relay
  • Recruiter/CV emails with HTML resumes
Suppression filter
| where SenderFromDomain !in~ ("news.example-vendor.com") and not (SenderMailFromDomain endswith "mailchimpapp.net")
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the HTML attachment in a sandbox and grep for CSS @import, invisible <svg onload>, atob()/Base64 blobs, or references to the nine campaign domains. 2) Contain: if malicious, purge the message tenant-wide (soft-delete), block sender + any embedded domain, and reset the recipient's Zimbra credentials and 2FA scratch codes. 3) Hunt: confirm whether the org runs unpatched ZCS, and correlate the recipient against the C2-contact detection to see if exfiltration already occurred.

| project TimeGenerated, SenderFromAddress, SenderMailFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, FileName, FileType, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, ThreatTypes
| order by TimeGenerated desc
Browser-initiated webmail data exfiltration to CL-STA-1114 C2 AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts a browser process (the authenticated Zimbra session) making outbound HTTP connections to the campaign's C2 domains/IPs — the moment the injected JavaScript ships stolen credentials, 2FA scratch codes, CSRF tokens and mail archive off-box. Distinct from the broad C2 detection by requiring a browser initiator and HTTP egress shape. Rationale: Combines the campaign's confirmed C2 IOC set with a browser-process initiator, because the CVE-2025-66376 XSS runs inside the authenticated Zimbra browser session and the injected JS exfiltrates over HTTP — a browser hitting these specific hosts is high-fidelity exfil, not generic web traffic. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa26-204a • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/russian-espionage-group-exploited.html
ATT&CKT1041 T1539 T1071.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Analyst manually browsing to a seized/sinkholed C2 domain during investigation
  • Browser prefetch/DNS-preconnect to a domain reclaimed post-takedown
  • URL-reputation browser extension pinging a look-alike domain
  • Sandbox/detonation VM using a real browser to detonate the sample
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--headless" and DeviceName !in~ ("SANDBOX-DETONATE01")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the browser had an active Zimbra session and inspect the request (POST body size, user-agent) for outbound credential/mail data. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block the C2 at proxy+DNS, and immediately reset the user's Zimbra password, 2FA scratch codes, and invalidate active sessions/CSRF tokens. 3) Hunt: sweep all browsers org-wide for the nine IOCs, review the mailbox for 90-day access anomalies, and check whether the org's ZCS is unpatched.

Unpatched Zimbra Collaboration exposed to CVE-2025-66376 (Void Blizzard target) AIExploitation High
Surfaces endpoints/servers running Zimbra Collaboration versions vulnerable to CVE-2025-66376 — the CSS @import stored-XSS that CL-STA-1114 exploits zero-click. Directly supports the article's proactive-patching guidance by listing exploitable ZCS instances (10.0<10.0.18, 10.1<10.1.13). Rationale: Uses the concrete CVE-2025-66376 identifier confirmed by SentinelOne's vuln database and the CISA advisory (affected 10.0<10.0.18 / 10.1<10.1.13); a TVM exposure hunt is the cleanest way to operationalize the article's patch-now message. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2025-66376/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/russian-hackers-exploit-zimbra-zero-click-flaw-for-email-theft/
ATT&CKT1203 T1190
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • ZCS host already patched to 10.0.18/10.1.13 but TVM inventory lagging a scan cycle
  • Non-production/staging Zimbra instance not internet-exposed
  • Defender TVM mis-parsing a Zimbra build string on a Linux appliance
  • Decommissioned server still present in the device inventory
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the Zimbra build and whether the Classic Web Client (the vulnerable UI) is enabled and internet-reachable. 2) Contain: schedule emergency patch to 10.0.18 / 10.1.13, or disable the Classic UI / apply the vendor mitigation in the interim. 3) Hunt: on each exposed host, correlate against the C2-contact and browser-exfil detections and review Zimbra mail logs for crafted HTML messages abusing CSS @import.

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files

DeviceEvents

                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")

          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName

// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.

| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663

| where Account !endswith "$"

          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

High

Chaos ransomware's msaRAT: Living off the browser to build a covert C2 channel

Cisco Talos
2026-07-23 10 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

The Chaos ransomware group uses new malware "msaRAT" that hijacks browsers. The malware doesn't communicate directly with C2 but connects through the browser. It enables arbitrary command execution while hiding the attacker's IP from victims via WebRTC over TURN.

ATT&CKT1190 T1204.002 T1486
Domainsis-01-ast.ols-img-12.workers.dev
IPs172.86.126.18

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] msaRAT delivery: curl.exe fetching fake Windows-update MSI to ProgramData over HTTP
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Article-specific behavioural hunt — Chaos ransomware's msaRAT: Living off the browser to build a covert C2 channel
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] msaRAT msiexec executing update_ms.msi (fake Windows update custom action)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] msaRAT CDP abuse: headless Chrome/Edge with remote-debugging port spawned by non-browser parent · [LLM] msaRAT C2 network IOC: connection to 172.86.126.18 or is-01-ast.ols-img-12.workers.dev · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
msaRAT delivery: curl.exe fetching fake Windows-update MSI to ProgramData over HTTP AIDelivery High
Hunts the Chaos ransomware pre-ransom delivery step where curl.exe downloads update_ms.msi from attacker IP 172.86.126.18 (plain HTTP on port 443) into C:\programdata\. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact filename, ProgramData sink, and hardcoded C2 IP. Rationale: Anchors on the verbatim curl command from the article (update_ms.msi, hardcoded 172.86.126.18, ProgramData sink) — the triple combination is essentially unique to this campaign. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/chaos-msarat-living-off-the-browser-to-build-covert-c2-channel/ • https://malware.news/t/chaos-ransomwares-msarat-living-off-the-browser-to-build-a-covert-c2-channel/124199 • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/new-chaos-ransomware/
ATT&CKT1105 T1218.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate admin using curl to stage a genuine .msi in ProgramData for software deployment
  • Internal software-distribution tooling pulling installers over HTTP to a ProgramData staging folder
  • Security researcher / detection-lab replaying the sample
  • RMM agent using curl to fetch an update package into ProgramData
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("ansible.exe","chocolatey.exe","choco.exe") and ProcessCommandLine !has "172.86.126.18")
Response runbook
1. Triage: confirm the curl invocation targeted 172.86.126.18 or the literal update_ms.msi filename and that the destination is C:\programdata\; pull the parent process and user. 2. Contain: network-isolate the host, block 172.86.126.18 at the perimeter, and quarantine C:\programdata\update_ms.msi before it is executed by msiexec. 3. Hunt: pivot to msiexec execution of that MSI, headless Chrome/Edge launches with --remote-debugging-port, and outbound traffic to is-01-ast.ols-img-12.workers.dev across the estate.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "curl.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("update_ms.msi", "172.86.126.18")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "programdata"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetProcessName has "curl.exe"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("update_ms.msi", "172.86.126.18")
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "programdata"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Detects Chaos ransomware msaRAT delivery where curl.exe downloads update_ms.msi from attacker IP 172.86.126.18 into ProgramData

msaRAT msiexec executing update_ms.msi (fake Windows update custom action) AIInstallation High
Hunts the second delivery stage: msiexec.exe executing the Chaos-dropped update_ms.msi whose custom action CA_Run_EA2AEBC3 reflectively loads lib.dll (msaRAT) into memory. Article-specific via the exact MSI filename. Rationale: The MSI filename update_ms.msi is a verbatim article IOC; msiexec running it from ProgramData is the reflective-load launcher for lib.dll (msaRAT). Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/chaos-msarat-living-off-the-browser-to-build-covert-c2-channel/ • https://malware.news/t/chaos-ransomwares-msarat-living-off-the-browser-to-build-a-covert-c2-channel/124199
ATT&CKT1218.007 T1620
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate vendor MSI happening to be staged in ProgramData (broad hunting clause only)
  • Patch-management tool invoking msiexec against a ProgramData-cached installer
  • Detection-lab replay of the sample
  • Third-party updater masquerading its own installer in ProgramData
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine has "update_ms.msi"
Response runbook
1. Triage: confirm msiexec ran update_ms.msi and inspect the MSI's Binary table / custom action (CA_Run_*) and file metadata spoofing Microsoft. 2. Contain: isolate the host, kill any child processes, and capture memory before reboot to recover the in-memory lib.dll (msaRAT). 3. Hunt: look for the RUN export being invoked, headless browser launches with CDP flags, and workers.dev / Twilio TURN egress to confirm C2 establishment.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "msiexec.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "update_ms.msi" or (ProcessCommandLine has @"\programdata\" and ProcessCommandLine has ".msi")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetProcessName has "msiexec.exe"

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

msaRAT CDP abuse: headless Chrome/Edge with remote-debugging port spawned by non-browser parent AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts msaRAT's core 'living off the browser' behaviour — it launches Chrome or Edge in headless mode with the CDP remote-debugging port enabled to drive a covert WebRTC C2. Detects browsers started with --headless/--remote-debugging-port by a parent that is not a browser or explorer. Rationale: The article states msaRAT launches Chrome/Edge headless via CreateProcessW with the remote-debugging port enabled to drive CDP; a browser started with these flags by a non-browser parent is a high-fidelity signal of programmatic browser hijack. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/chaos-msarat-living-off-the-browser-to-build-covert-c2-channel/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/chaes-malware-now-uses-google-chrome-devtools-protocol-to-steal-data/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1572 T1218.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Puppeteer/Playwright/Selenium automation frameworks launching headless Chrome for testing
  • CI/CD build agents rendering pages via headless Chrome
  • PDF-generation or web-scraping services spawning headless browsers
  • Developer tooling that opens Chrome with --remote-debugging-port for debugging
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("node.exe", "python.exe", "pwsh.exe", "cypress.exe", "chromedriver.exe", "selenium.exe")
Response runbook
1. Triage: identify the parent process that spawned the headless browser and the remote-debugging port; benign automation parents (node.exe from a known project) differ sharply from msiexec/rundll32/unknown. 2. Contain: if the parent is unexpected, isolate the host and terminate the browser + parent process tree, then capture memory for the in-memory msaRAT. 3. Hunt: correlate with the CDP JSON endpoint (/json/list/) on localhost, WebRTC/TURN egress to global.turn.twilio.com, and connections to is-01-ast.ols-img-12.workers.dev.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine

    Image|endswith:
      - '\chrome.exe'
      - '\msedge.exe'

| CommandLine=/--(remote-debugging-port|headless)/i

| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

msaRAT C2 network IOC: connection to 172.86.126.18 or is-01-ast.ols-img-12.workers.dev AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts network contact with the hardcoded Chaos/msaRAT infrastructure — the delivery IP 172.86.126.18 and the Cloudflare Workers signaling endpoint is-01-ast.ols-img-12.workers.dev used for SDP/ICE exchange. Pure IOC pivot corroborated by Talos. Rationale: Both the delivery IP 172.86.126.18 and the Cloudflare Workers signaling domain are verbatim, campaign-unique IOCs from the Talos article; direct network matches are the highest-fidelity possible pivot. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/chaos-msarat-living-off-the-browser-to-build-covert-c2-channel/ • https://malware.news/t/chaos-ransomwares-msarat-living-off-the-browser-to-build-a-covert-c2-channel/124199

Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment

  • Threat-intel sandbox / detonation VM deliberately contacting the IOC
  • Security scanner probing the attacker IP
  • Proxy log replay from an IR investigation
Response runbook
1. Triage: identify the source host and the initiating process (a browser process for the workers.dev signaling, or curl/msiexec for the delivery IP). 2. Contain: block 172.86.126.18 and is-01-ast.ols-img-12.workers.dev at the firewall/proxy and network-isolate the source host. 3. Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT

    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200

LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access

    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT

        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:

    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02

    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT

       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")

          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

Preview: Cisco Talos at Black Hat USA 2026

Cisco Talos
2026-07-23 3 use cases 2 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Here’s some of the ways Talos is showing up at Black Hat, alongside our friends at Cisco and Splunk.

ATT&CKT1190 T1486
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
—
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
—
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Likely
Phase 6
Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Shai-Hulud / GhostAction malicious workflow artifact dropped on runner or repo checkout
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Network egress from CI/build host to GhostAction secret-exfil infrastructure · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] CI runner process POSTing secrets to GhostAction exfil host via curl/wget/node · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
Network egress from CI/build host to GhostAction secret-exfil infrastructure AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts outbound connections from self-hosted GitHub Actions runners (or any host) to the GhostAction campaign's exfiltration infrastructure — the 45.139.104.115 / 216.126.225.129 IPs and the bold-dhawan/objective-hopper .plesk.page and carte-avantage.com domains that received 3,325+ stolen Actions secrets via curl POST. Pure IOC match on confirmed campaign infra, so any hit is a near-certain secret-dump in flight. Rationale: Uses the article's pre-extracted GhostAction IPs (45.139.104.115, 216.126.225.129) and domains (bold-dhawan/objective-hopper.plesk.page, carte-avantage.com), all independently confirmed by GitGuardian/BleepingComputer as the campaign's secret-exfil endpoints — hard IOCs, near-zero benign contact. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign-3-325-secrets-stolen/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/hackers-steal-3-325-secrets-in-ghostaction-github-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
ATT&CKT1567 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher or malware sandbox intentionally connecting to the IOC hosts for validation
  • Threat-intel enrichment/beaconing-check tooling that resolves or pings known-bad domains
  • Firewall logging a blocked/denied connection attempt (still worth reviewing, not a true compromise)
  • A future benign tenant reusing the 45-139-104-115.plesk.page shared-hosting range after takedown
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","SenseIR.exe","MsMpEng.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the source host — confirm whether it is a self-hosted GitHub Actions runner or CI build agent; pull the initiating process and command line. 2) Contain: block the IOC IPs/domains at egress, isolate the runner, and immediately rotate every secret that runner had access to (npm/PyPI/DockerHub/cloud tokens). 3) Hunt: enumerate all repos whose workflows executed on that runner, review recent .github/workflows commits for injected exfil steps, and pivot to UC2/UC3 on the same host.
let iocIPs = dynamic(["45.139.104.115","216.126.225.129"]);
let iocDomains = dynamic(["bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page","objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page","carte-avantage.com"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteIP in (iocIPs) or RemoteUrl has_any (iocDomains)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

Shai-Hulud / GhostAction malicious workflow artifact dropped on runner or repo checkout AIDelivery Medium
Hunts the file-write of the Shai-Hulud worm's signature workflow (shai-hulud-workflow.yml) and its bundle.js payload inside a repo/.github/workflows or runner working directory, plus the article's SHA1 acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038. These are the planted supply-chain artifacts that harvest GitHub Actions secrets; the filename + hash are highly specific. Rationale: Keys on the Shai-Hulud worm's confirmed artifact name shai-hulud-workflow.yml (ReversingLabs/Datadog) plus the article's own SHA1 acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038, scoping bundle.js to CI/runner paths to keep it high-fidelity for the supply-chain drop the article warns about. Cross-checked against: • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/shai-hulud-worm-npm • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1204.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A researcher cloning a compromised repo into a lab machine for analysis
  • EDR/AV quarantine or backup software creating a copy of the malicious file (write event on a security tool path)
  • A remediation/scanner tool writing the IOC filename as a signature sample
  • A developer intentionally checking in a same-named benign workflow (rare given the exact name)
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsMpEng.exe","MsSense.exe","veeam.backup.shell.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the writing process (git, node, runner agent) and the repo/path; check whether the workflow already executed on this host. 2) Contain: isolate the runner, remove/quarantine the file, and rotate all secrets the affected repo/runner could read (npm publishing tokens first — Shai-Hulud reuses them to publish trojanized packages). 3) Hunt: search the org's repos for the same filename/hash and for recent commits by unfamiliar contributors adding .github/workflows files across many repos in a short window.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "shai-hulud-workflow.yml"
   or SHA1 == "acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038"
   or (FileName =~ "bundle.js" and FolderPath has_any (@"\.github\", @"\_work\", @"\actions-runner\", "/.github/", "/_work/"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "shai-hulud-workflow.yml"
   or TargetFileSHA1 =~ "acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038"
   or (TargetFileName =~ "bundle.js" and TargetFilePath has_any (@"\.github\", "/.github/", "/_work/", @"\_work\"))
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA1
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CI runner process POSTing secrets to GhostAction exfil host via curl/wget/node AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts a process on a self-hosted runner or build host invoking curl/wget/node/powershell with the GhostAction exfil hosts embedded in the command line — the on-runner footprint of the injected workflow step that curl-POSTs the harvested secret context to attacker infrastructure. Combines execution telemetry with confirmed campaign IOCs in the invocation string. Rationale: Matches the GhostAction exfil hosts appearing directly in a process command line — the runner-side signature of the curl POST that GitGuardian documented shipping secrets to these domains; command-line IOC presence is high-fidelity regardless of the interpreter used. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign-3-325-secrets-stolen/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/hackers-steal-3-325-secrets-in-ghostaction-github-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1059 T1567 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC analyst manually running curl against the IOC host to validate takedown/sinkhole status
  • Threat-intel enrichment or connectivity-check script that references the IOC in its arguments
  • Detection-engineering test/purple-team replay of the GhostAction payload in a lab
  • Backup or copy operation that includes the IOC string in a filename argument
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","SenseIR.exe") and AccountName !endswith "$"
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full command line and parent process; determine if this ran inside a workflow step on a runner vs an interactive analyst session. 2) Contain: kill/isolate the host, block the IOC hosts at egress, and rotate every secret exposed to that workflow run (prioritize npm/PyPI publishing tokens to prevent downstream package poisoning). 3) Hunt: correlate with UC1 network hits on the same host, review the triggering workflow's git history for the injected curl step, and check package registries for unexpected publishes using the potentially stolen tokens.

| order by TimeGenerated desc

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or SHA1 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or MD5 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or SHA1 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or MD5 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

NodeBB remote federated-user profile lookup (ActivityPub XSS #1 trigger) AIDelivery Medium
Hunts inbound requests to NodeBB's /user/<name>@<domain> route, which forces the server to webfinger-resolve and fetch a remote ActivityPub actor whose icon.url is rendered unescaped into an og:image <meta> tag (buildMetaTag sink). A victim clicking such a link to an attacker-controlled instance is the delivery vector for the reflected XSS fixed in NodeBB 4.14.0. Surfaces which external handles/domains are being resolved so an analyst can spot lures to attacker.tld / ephemeral tunnel instances. Rationale: Anchored on the exact NodeBB federation route /user/<name>@<domain> from the article's PoC (https://nodebb.local/user/xss@attacker.tld) — the sole inbound signal that forces the server to fetch and render a remote actor's unescaped icon.url. Handles both raw '@' and URL-encoded '%40'. Cross-checked against: • https://community.nodebb.org/topic/19387/nodebb-v4.14.0-federation-regression-fixes-security-fixes-and-more • https://github.com/NodeBB/NodeBB/security/advisories/GHSA-wx69-rvg3-x7fc
ATT&CKT1189 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate federation: real users browsing genuine remote Fediverse/NodeBB/Lemmy actors (e.g. someuser@mastodon.social)
  • Search-engine or link-preview crawlers hitting shared federated profile URLs
  • Fediverse relay/aggregator bots resolving remote handles
  • Admins testing federation against known-good partner instances
  • Users pasting handles copied from other platforms
Suppression filter
| where RemoteHandleDomain !in~ ("mastodon.social","lemmy.world","lemmy.ml")   // extend with your org's known-good federation partners
Response runbook
1) Triage: extract RemoteHandleDomain from the hits; check whether the domain is a known federation partner or a newly-seen/ephemeral host (e.g. *.trycloudflare.com, freshly-registered). Correlate the client IP/user who requested it. 2) Contain: if the target NodeBB is <4.14.0, expedite upgrade (fix commit 4c4bf76 escapes federated data); temporarily disable ActivityPub or block outbound resolution to the suspicious domain at the egress proxy. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC 'Outbound ActivityPub actor fetch' for the same domain, and review admin session activity for any account whose browser rendered the profile (session hijack via alert(origin) equivalent).
AppServiceHTTPLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where CsUriStem startswith "/user/"
| where CsUriStem contains "@" or CsUriStem contains "%40"
| extend RemoteHandleDomain = extract(@"/user/[^/@]+(?:@|%40)([A-Za-z0-9._-]+)", 1, CsUriStem)
| where isnotempty(RemoteHandleDomain)
| summarize Requests = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), Clients = make_set(CIp, 20) by RemoteHandleDomain, CsUriStem, CsHost
| order by FirstSeen desc

NodeBB server outbound ActivityPub/webfinger fetch to ephemeral tunnel or new domain AIExploitation Medium
Detects the NodeBB server making outbound /.well-known/webfinger and /ap/actor ActivityPub discovery requests to attacker-controlled federation instances — the server-side fetch that pulls the malicious actor JSON (icon.url XSS payload) or a rogue signing key. Flags ephemeral Cloudflare tunnel destinations (observed in the research setup) and federation partners never seen before in the baseline window. Rationale: Uses the article's concrete ActivityPub discovery paths (/.well-known/webfinger, /ap/actor/) plus the pre-extracted ephemeral-tunnel IOC (*.trycloudflare.com, the host attacker.tld was fronted by) and a newly-seen-partner anti-join. Server-side outbound fetch is the pivot that distinguishes exploitation from normal browsing. Cross-checked against: • https://community.nodebb.org/topic/19387/nodebb-v4.14.0-federation-regression-fixes-security-fixes-and-more
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1102
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate first-time federation with a new but benign remote instance
  • Developers/researchers self-hosting a test Fediverse instance on a Cloudflare tunnel
  • Relay/Lemmy federation resuming after the 4.14.0 upgrade contacting many new hosts at once
  • Link-unfurl or preview services proxied through the same egress path
  • Security scanners probing the org's ActivityPub endpoints
Suppression filter
| where DstHostname !in~ ("mastodon.social","lemmy.world","lemmy.ml")   // known-good federation partners
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the source is the NodeBB host and the destination is not a sanctioned federation partner; resolve the domain age/hosting (ephemeral tunnels + <30-day domains are suspicious). 2) Contain: block egress to the destination host and, if the icon.url XSS is unpatched (<4.14.0), disable ActivityPub until upgraded. 3) Hunt: correlate with UC1 inbound /user/<h>@<domain> requests for the same domain, and check admin/moderator sessions rendered around the fetch time for token/session anomalies indicating a successful stored/reflected XSS.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

NodeBB ActivityPub inbox POST carrying HTML-breakout id (Federation Errors stored XSS) AIExploitation Low
Detects unauthenticated POSTs to the NodeBB ActivityPub inbox where the request body's actor/object 'id' contains HTML metacharacters ( "> < img onerror ). Per the article, inbox.js processes signed Update activities and logs failures into the admin Federation Errors view where {./id} is emitted with auto-escaping disabled (identity function), so a crafted id stores XSS that fires when an administrator opens the log. Requires WAF/proxy request-body capture. Rationale: Anchored on the article's second sink: inbox.js handling signed Update activities and the unescaped {./id} in errors.tpl (Benchpress __escape replaced with identity). Matches POST to the /inbox path plus HTML-breakout tokens in the captured body/query. Confidence Low because it depends on WAF body logging. Cross-checked against: • https://community.nodebb.org/topic/19387/nodebb-v4.14.0-federation-regression-fixes-security-fixes-and-more
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1211
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Benign remote instances whose actor id legitimately contains encoded characters flagged by the naive payload match
  • WAF already normalizing/decoding bodies causing double-encoded false hits
  • Federation debugging tools posting malformed test activities
  • Security scanners fuzzing the ActivityPub inbox
  • Relay software posting large batched activities that trip the substring match
Response runbook
1) Triage: decode the request body and confirm the actor/object 'id' actually contains a script/HTML-breakout ( "><img onerror ) rather than benign encoding; note the source instance and signing key. 2) Contain: if NodeBB <4.14.0, do NOT open the admin Federation Errors page from a privileged browser session (it would fire the stored payload); upgrade first, then purge stored federation error entries. 3) Hunt: check whether any admin/global-moderator account viewed the Federation Errors panel after the malicious POST, and review those sessions and admin actions for XSS-driven account or CSRF-style changes.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestMethod =~ "POST"
| where RequestURL has "/inbox"
| where Message has_any ("onerror", "<img", "\">", "%22%3E", "&quot;&gt;") or RequestContext has_any ("onerror", "<img", "\">")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestMethod, RequestClientApplication, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc

fast16 sabotage implant carrier (svcmgmt.exe) by hash / Lua-carrier behaviour AIInstallation High
Hunts the fast16 host carrier module svcmgmt.exe by its known file hashes and by its distinctive behaviour of running as a service and interpreting embedded Lua 5.0 script arguments. Targets the specific 2005 nuclear-solver sabotage toolkit re-analysed by SentinelLABS, not a generic service binary. Rationale: Anchored on the three SHA256/SHA1/MD5 hashes of the fast16 toolkit plus the carrier binary svcmgmt.exe, whose embedded Lua 5.0 VM and service-carrier behaviour are corroborated by SecurityWeek and the original SentinelLABS fast16 write-up. Hash matches are zero-FP; the svcmgmt.exe+Lua clause adds behavioural coverage for renamed/recompiled variants. Cross-checked against: • https://www.securityweek.com/pre-stuxnet-sabotage-malware-fast16-linked-to-us-iran-cyber-tensions/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/fast16-malware-with-sabotage-capabilities/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/fast16-mystery-shadowbrokers-reference-reveals-high-precision-software-sabotage-5-years-before-stuxnet/
ATT&CKT1569.002 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate third-party or line-of-business service binary coincidentally also named svcmgmt.exe (generic name) triggering the filename clause
  • Security-research or sandbox hosts intentionally detonating the fast16 sample for analysis (e.g. SentinelLABS-style benchmarking)
  • AV/EDR quarantine or IR-collection tooling copying the sample, surfacing the hash in scan telemetry
  • A malware-analyst workstation with the sample in a triage folder that gets executed under a debugger
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName has_any ("SANDBOX","MALWARE","IR-") and SHA256 !in~ (fast16_sha256))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the match is on file hash (definitive) vs the svcmgmt.exe filename clause; check FolderPath, signer, and whether the host is a known malware-analysis/sandbox box. 2) Contain: if hash-confirmed on a production host, network-isolate the endpoint, preserve the binary and any dropped .sys/.lua artifacts, and snapshot memory before the driver can re-patch target software. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same hosts and any that share the file for the fast16.sys driver load (UC2), for high-precision engineering software (LS-DYNA, ANSYS AUTODYN, PKPM, MOHID) present on the box, and for lateral self-propagation of svcmgmt.exe to peers.
let fast16_sha256 = dynamic(["9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525","07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529","8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9"]);
let fast16_md5 = dynamic(["dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944","0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26","410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449"]);
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(90d)
| where SHA256 in~ (fast16_sha256)
   or MD5 in~ (fast16_md5)
   or (FileName =~ "svcmgmt.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has_any (".lua","lua","fast16"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, MD5,
          ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
let fast16_sha256 = dynamic(["9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525","07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529","8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9"]);
let fast16_md5 = dynamic(["dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944","0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26","410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449"]);

| where SHA256 in~ (fast16_sha256)
   or MD5 in~ (fast16_md5)
   or (TargetProcessName has "svcmgmt.exe" and TargetProcessCommandLine has_any (".lua","lua","fast16"))

          TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
