fast16 kernel driver (fast16.sys) drop / load — sabotage patching engine AIInstallation High
Hunts for the fast16.sys kernel driver — the component that hosts the 101-rule in-memory patching engine used to tamper with high-precision solver output. Detects the driver being written to disk or loaded, a rare and highly specific artifact of the fast16 sabotage toolkit. Rationale: fast16.sys is the uniquely-named kernel driver corroborated by SecurityWeek and CyberSecurityNews as the fast16 component embedding the in-memory patching engine that tampers with solver calculations; a file/driver by that exact name is effectively pathognomonic for the toolkit with negligible legitimate collision. Cross-checked against: • https://www.securityweek.com/pre-stuxnet-sabotage-malware-fast16-linked-to-us-iran-cyber-tensions/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/fast16-malware-with-sabotage-capabilities/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/fast16-mystery-shadowbrokers-reference-reveals-high-precision-software-sabotage-5-years-before-stuxnet/
ATT&CKT1014 T1543.003 T1565
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A malware-analysis/reverse-engineering workstation intentionally loading the fast16.sys sample (e.g. the SentinelLABS benchmark environment)
  • An unrelated third-party driver coincidentally named fast16.sys (extremely unlikely given the naming)
  • EDR/AV extracting or quarantining the .sys and re-writing it to a quarantine path, surfacing a FileWritten event
  • IR imaging/collection copying the driver artifact off a compromised host
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName has_any ("SANDBOX","MALWARE","RE-LAB"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: verify the FolderPath and the driver's signature/SHA256; determine whether the host is an analysis sandbox or a production/engineering workstation. 2) Contain: on any production host, isolate the endpoint immediately, block driver load via ASR/WDAC where possible, and preserve the .sys plus any Lua scripts and svcmgmt.exe carrier for forensic imaging before the patching engine mutates target-software memory. 3) Hunt: enumerate high-precision solvers on the host (LS-DYNA, ANSYS AUTODYN, PKPM, MOHID) for evidence of in-memory patching or corrupted results, correlate with UC1 svcmgmt.exe execution, and sweep the facility subnet for the same driver name (self-propagation).
DeviceImageLoadEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(90d)
| where FileName =~ "fast16.sys"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| union (
    DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(90d)
    | where FileName =~ "fast16.sys"
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
              InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
  )
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "fast16.sys"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256, DvcAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High

DeviceProcessEvents

| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"

          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"

| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449") or SHA1 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449") or MD5 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449") or SHA1 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449") or MD5 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

SQL injection isn't dead

Aikido
2026-07-22 4 use cases 2 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

The fix for SQL injection is decades old and still works. So why did WordPress core just need an emergency patch for one? The data, and how to defend against it. Category: News

CVEsCVE-2026-60137 CVE-2026-63030 CVE-2024-42005
ATT&CKT1021.001 T1190
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] wp2shell SQLi via author__not_in REST parameter (CVE-2026-60137) · [LLM] WordPress web-server/PHP process spawning a shell (wp2shell RCE)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Malicious PHP plugin/webshell dropped in wp-content by web server (wp2shell)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
wp2shell SQLi via author__not_in REST parameter (CVE-2026-60137) AIExploitation High
Hunts WordPress REST requests where the author__not_in parameter (normally an integer ID array) carries SQL-injection content, the core signature of CVE-2026-60137 exploited through the batch-route desync in the wp2shell chain. Targets the initial unauthenticated exploitation step against /wp-json/wp/v2/posts. Rationale: author__not_in is documented (Rapid7/VulnCheck) to accept only integer author IDs; any SQL metacharacter in it is the CVE-2026-60137 injection signature. Note: PoCs delivered inside a POST /wp-json/batch/v1 body won't appear in access-log query strings — pair with WAF body inspection (CommonSecurityLog RequestURL/DeviceCustomString) to catch batch-wrapped payloads. Defender/Falcon/Datadog/CloudWatch left empty: WordPress web-request telemetry does not live in EDR/CloudTrail. Cross-checked against: • https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/ • https://www.tenable.com/blog/wp2shell-cve-2026-63030-cve-2026-60137-frequently-asked-questions-about-remote-code-execution • https://www.vulncheck.com/blog/wp2shell • https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/wp2shell-millions-wordpress-sites-remote-takeover
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized vulnerability scanners (WPScan, Nessus, Qualys) sending SQLi test strings to /wp-json/wp/v2/posts
  • Security researchers / bug-bounty testers replaying the public wp2shell PoC against your own estate
  • Legitimate REST clients that URL-encode a comma-separated author list where an ID happens to contain a matched token (rare)
  • WAF-decoded double requests logging both the encoded and decoded query, doubling alert volume
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !has_any ("WPScan","Nessus","Qualys","Nuclei","sqlmap") and cIP !in ("<your_scanner_ips>")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Pull all requests from the source IP; confirm author__not_in carries SQL (quotes, UNION, SLEEP, information_schema) rather than integers, and note HTTP status — a 200 on a time-based/UNION probe indicates successful injection. 2) CONTAIN: Block the source IP at the WAF/edge and confirm the site is on WordPress 7.0.2 / 6.9.5 / 6.8.6; if unpatched, force the emergency update immediately (WordPress.org enabled forced auto-updates for this). 3) HUNT: Pivot to the same host for post-exploit activity — new administrator accounts, PHP files written under wp-content/plugins or wp-content/uploads, and web-server/PHP processes spawning shells (see companion UCs).
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csUriStem has "wp-json" or csUriQuery has "rest_route"
| where csUriQuery has "author__not_in"
| where csUriQuery has_any ("'","%27","union","select","sleep","information_schema","0x","--","/*")
| project TimeGenerated, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent, Computer
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: WordPress wp2shell SQL Injection via author__not_in Parameter (CVE-2026-60137)
id: 8f2c1a44-3e5d-4b6a-9c2f-1a7b9d0e4c11
description: Detects SQL-injection content in the WordPress REST author__not_in parameter, which normally only accepts integer post-author IDs. Core signature of the wp2shell pre-auth RCE chain.
references:
  - https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/
  - https://www.tenable.com/blog/wp2shell-cve-2026-63030-cve-2026-60137-frequently-asked-questions-about-remote-code-execution
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/22
tags:

  - attack.initial_access
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection_param:
    cs-uri-query|contains: 'author__not_in'
  selection_sqli:
    cs-uri-query|contains:
      - "'"
      - '%27'
      - 'union'
      - 'select'
      - 'sleep'
      - 'information_schema'
      - '0x'
      - '--'
      - '/*'
  condition: selection_param and selection_sqli
falsepositives:
  - Security scanners (Nessus, Qualys, WPScan) probing the endpoint
level: high

| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Web.status) as status values(Web.http_method) as http_method from datamodel=Web where Web.url="*author__not_in*" AND (Web.url="*'*" OR Web.url="*%27*" OR Web.url="*union*" OR Web.url="*UNION*" OR Web.url="*select*" OR Web.url="*SELECT*" OR Web.url="*sleep*" OR Web.url="*information_schema*" OR Web.url="*0x*" OR Web.url="*--*" OR Web.url="*/*") by Web.src Web.dest Web.http_user_agent Web.url | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | sort - lastTime

WordPress web-server/PHP process spawning a shell (wp2shell RCE) AIExploitation High
Detects the code-execution stage of wp2shell where the web-request handler (php-fpm, php-cgi, w3wp, apache/nginx) spawns a command shell or download utility — behaviour a normal WordPress worker never exhibits. Confirms the SQLi chain has reached remote code execution. Rationale: The wp2shell chain culminates in uploading a malicious plugin for code execution; that code runs under the PHP/web-server process, so a web worker spawning cmd/sh/whoami/curl is a direct RCE confirmation. Datadog uses windows.sysmon (IIS/PHP-on-Windows); Linux hosts should use source:linux.auditd EXECVE with the same process names. Cross-checked against: • https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/ • https://cyberinsider.com/wordpress-releases-emergency-update-for-critical-wp2shell-rce-flaw/ • https://www.indusface.com/blog/wp2shell-wordpress-cve-2026-60137-63030-rce/
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Backup plugins (UpdraftPlus, BackWPup) shelling out to mysqldump/gzip/tar
  • Image-optimisation plugins invoking imagemagick 'convert' or 'jpegoptim' via PHP exec()
  • wp-cron running PHP CLI jobs that legitimately call curl to fetch feed data
  • Server-monitoring agents that fork sh from the PHP-FPM pool for health checks
  • Composer/WP-CLI maintenance run interactively on the web host by an admin
Suppression filter
| where not (FileName in~ ("sh","bash") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("mysqldump","imagemagick","convert","jpegoptim","wp-cron.php","backwpup","updraft"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Inspect the child ProcessCommandLine — reconnaissance (whoami, id, uname), outbound fetch (curl/wget to an unknown host), or an interactive shell are strong TP signals; confirm the parent PHP worker is serving the WordPress vhost. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, kill the offending process tree, and rotate WordPress DB and admin credentials; take a forensic copy of wp-content before remediation. 3) HUNT: Enumerate all administrator accounts for rogue additions, list recently-modified PHP files under wp-content/plugins and wp-content/uploads, and review web logs for the preceding author__not_in / batch/v1 exploitation requests.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("php-cgi.exe","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp.exe","httpd.exe","httpd","apache2","nginx.exe","nginx")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","sh","bash","dash","whoami.exe","whoami","curl.exe","curl","wget","python.exe","python3")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("php-cgi","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp","httpd","apache2","nginx")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell","pwsh","/sh","/bash","/dash","whoami","curl","wget","python")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: WordPress Web-Server/PHP Process Spawning Shell (wp2shell RCE)
id: 2b9e5c17-6d41-4a8e-b3c0-7f21a9e4d502
description: Web-request handler (php-fpm/php-cgi/w3wp/apache/nginx) spawning a command interpreter or download tool, indicating web-shell command execution from the wp2shell chain.
references:
  - https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/
  - https://cyberinsider.com/wordpress-releases-emergency-update-for-critical-wp2shell-rce-flaw/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/22
tags:
  - attack.t1505.003
  - attack.t1059
  - attack.execution
logsource:
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\php-cgi.exe'
      - '\php.exe'
      - '\w3wp.exe'
      - '/php-fpm'
      - '/php'
      - '/apache2'
      - '/httpd'
      - '/nginx'
    Image|endswith:
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
      - '/sh'
      - '/bash'
      - '/dash'
      - '\whoami.exe'
      - '/whoami'
      - '/curl'
      - '/wget'
      - '/python3'
  condition: selection
falsepositives:
  - Backup/media plugins invoking imagemagick, mysqldump or curl via shell
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\php-cgi.exe OR *\\php.exe OR *\\w3wp.exe OR *\\W3WP.exe) @Image:(*\\cmd.exe OR *\\powershell.exe OR *\\PowerShell.exe OR *\\pwsh.exe)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ParentBaseFileName=/^(php-cgi|php|php-fpm|w3wp|httpd|apache2|nginx)(\.exe)?$/i
| ImageFileName=/(cmd|powershell|pwsh|sh|bash|dash|whoami|curl|wget|python3?)(\.exe)?$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT

Malicious PHP plugin/webshell dropped in wp-content by web server (wp2shell) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts PHP files written into wp-content/plugins or wp-content/uploads by the web-server/PHP process — the persistence/execution payload the wp2shell chain uploads after minting a rogue admin. Distinguishes attacker-dropped web shells from normal user-initiated plugin installs by anchoring on the writing process and target path. Rationale: The documented wp2shell escalation uploads a malicious plugin for code execution; a PHP file written into wp-content/plugins or the uploads dir by the PHP/web-server process is the on-disk artefact of that step. Anchored on both the target path and writing process to separate attacker drops from normal user-driven plugin installs. Cross-checked against: • https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/ • https://www.vulncheck.com/blog/wp2shell • https://www.tenable.com/blog/wp2shell-cve-2026-63030-cve-2026-60137-frequently-asked-questions-about-remote-code-execution
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1505
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Administrator installing or updating a legitimate plugin from the WordPress dashboard (writes many .php files to wp-content/plugins)
  • Automatic plugin/theme auto-updates writing PHP during maintenance windows
  • Caching plugins (W3 Total Cache, WP Rocket) generating advanced-cache.php or object-cache.php
  • Media/gallery plugins writing helper PHP into wp-content/uploads
  • WP-CLI 'wp plugin install' run by an operator on the host
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("wp plugin install","wp plugin update","advanced-cache.php","object-cache.php") or FolderPath has_any ("w3-total-cache","wp-rocket"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Read the dropped PHP — short obfuscated files or those containing eval/base64_decode/system/assert/$_POST[ are web shells; a single .php landing directly in wp-content/uploads is almost never legitimate. Check whether the write coincides with a dashboard plugin install by a known admin. 2) CONTAIN: Quarantine the file, hash and submit it, and isolate the host if it is a web shell; revoke sessions and reset admin/DB credentials. 3) HUNT: Correlate the write time backward to author__not_in/batch/v1 exploitation in web logs and forward to web-server processes spawning shells or outbound C2, and audit wp_users for a rogue administrator created just before the drop.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has "wp-content" and (FolderPath has "plugins" or FolderPath has "uploads")
| where FileName endswith ".php"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("php-cgi.exe","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp.exe","httpd.exe","httpd","nginx.exe","nginx","apache2")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where TargetFilePath has "wp-content" and (TargetFilePath has "plugins" or TargetFilePath has "uploads")
| where TargetFileName endswith ".php"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("php-cgi","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp","httpd","apache2","nginx")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Malicious PHP Written to WordPress wp-content by Web Server (wp2shell)
id: c4a7e213-9f08-4d55-8b1e-6ad2c0f79b34
description: PHP file created under wp-content/plugins or wp-content/uploads by a web-server/PHP process, consistent with the wp2shell post-exploit malicious-plugin/web-shell drop.
references:
  - https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/
  - https://www.vulncheck.com/blog/wp2shell
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/22
tags:
  - attack.t1505.003
  - attack.persistence
logsource:
  category: file_event
detection:
  selection_path:
    TargetFilename|contains:
      - 'wp-content/plugins/'
      - 'wp-content\plugins\'
      - 'wp-content/uploads/'
      - 'wp-content\uploads\'
  selection_ext:
    TargetFilename|endswith: '.php'
  condition: selection_path and selection_ext
falsepositives:
  - Legitimate plugin install/update from the WordPress admin dashboard
  - Media plugins caching generated PHP in uploads
level: medium
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @TargetFilename:(*wp-content*plugins*.php OR *wp-content*uploads*.php) @Image:(*\\php-cgi.exe OR *\\php.exe OR *\\w3wp.exe OR *\\W3WP.exe)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten
| TargetFileName=/wp-content[\/\\](plugins|uploads)[\/\\].*\.php$/i
| ContextImageFileName=/(php-cgi|php|php-fpm|w3wp|httpd|apache2|nginx)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName, ContextImageFileName], function=count())

The upgrade trap: when upgrading is the wrong answer to a CVE

Aikido
2026-07-22 5 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

Upgrading to fix a CVE sounds straightforward. But the patched version often breaks your app, hasn't shipped yet, or doesn't exist. Here's why, and what actually works. Category: News

CVEsCVE-2026-48937
ATT&CKT1190
Domainsnpmjs.help
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
Detected
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Likely
Phase 6
Command & Control
Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Inbound phishing email or URL referencing npm look-alike domain npmjs.help · [LLM] Endpoint DNS resolution or web connection to npm phishing domain npmjs.help
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Inbound phishing email or URL referencing npm look-alike domain npmjs.help AIDelivery High
Hunts inbound mail whose sender domain or embedded URL uses the typosquat domain npmjs.help, the exact lure infrastructure used to phish the npm maintainer of chalk/debug and hijack their account. Any mail touching this domain is credential-harvest phishing, not a legitimate npm communication (real npm uses npmjs.com / github.com). Rationale: Anchored on the exact IOC npmjs.help (sender domain and embedded URL), the phishing infrastructure confirmed by Sygnia/StepSecurity/Semgrep as the vector that compromised the chalk & debug maintainer account. Sentinel left blank: this tenant's provided schema exposes no Microsoft Defender for Office 365 email tables (EmailEvents/EmailUrlInfo have no clean Sentinel equivalent; OfficeActivity does not carry sender/URL domain reliably). Datadog/Falcon/CloudWatch blank: FDR and these sources do not ship M365 mailflow telemetry. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sygnia.co/threat-reports-and-advisories/npm-supply-chain-attack-september-2025/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/20-popular-npm-packages-compromised-chalk-debug-strip-ansi-color-convert-wrap-ansi • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/chalk-debug-and-color-on-npm-compromised-in-new-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1598.003
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal purple-team / phishing-simulation platform deliberately spoofing npmjs.help to test user awareness
  • A user forwarding the phishing message to the SOC or a phishing-report mailbox (original npmjs.help URL still present in EmailUrlInfo)
  • Security-newsletter or threat-intel digest whose body links out to npmjs.help as a documented IOC (matches on EmailUrl but is benign reporting)
  • Managed-detection vendor sending an advisory that embeds the IOC domain
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: pull every recipient of mail matching npmjs.help via NetworkMessageId; determine DeliveryAction (Delivered vs Blocked) and whether any recipient is an npm/GitHub package maintainer or CI/CD service account. 2) CONTAIN: soft-delete/quarantine the message across all mailboxes (Explorer purge), block npmjs.help at mail gateway and web proxy, and force credential reset + re-registration of MFA for any recipient who opened the link. 3) HUNT: pivot to UrlClickEvents and DeviceNetworkEvents for npmjs.help to find users who actually reached the portal; audit npm/GitHub account activity (new tokens, published package versions) for any maintainer recipient.
let lookback = 90d;
union
(EmailEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
| where SenderFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help" or SenderMailFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, SenderFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, Indicator = SenderFromDomain, Source = "SenderDomain"),
(EmailUrlInfo
| where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
| where UrlDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain, Indicator = UrlDomain, Source = "EmailUrl")
| order by Timestamp desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
let lookback = 90d;
union
(EmailEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where SenderFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help" or SenderMailFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project TimeGenerated, NetworkMessageId, SenderFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, Indicator = SenderFromDomain, Source = "SenderDomain"),
(EmailUrlInfo
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where UrlDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project TimeGenerated, NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain, Indicator = UrlDomain, Source = "EmailUrl")
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Endpoint DNS resolution or web connection to npm phishing domain npmjs.help AIDelivery High
Detects any host resolving or connecting to npmjs.help, the credential-harvest portal impersonating npm support in the September 2025 chalk/debug supply-chain compromise. Reaching this domain means a user clicked the lure and hit the fake 2FA/login page; there is no legitimate reason for an enterprise host to contact it. Rationale: Single hard IOC (npmjs.help) confirmed by multiple second sources as the phishing portal that harvested the maintainer's npm credentials; token-aligned domain match on network/DNS telemetry is near-zero-FP because the domain has no legitimate business use. Sysmon field DestinationHostname enumerated in two casings per Datadog case-sensitivity; Falcon DomainName is lower-cased but regex /i keeps it robust. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sygnia.co/threat-reports-and-advisories/npm-supply-chain-attack-september-2025/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/20-popular-npm-packages-compromised-chalk-debug-strip-ansi-color-convert-wrap-ansi • https://vercel.com/blog/critical-npm-supply-chain-attack-response-september-8-2025
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1598.003
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC analyst or threat researcher manually browsing npmjs.help during IOC investigation from a corporate host
  • Malware-analysis sandbox / detonation VM resolving the domain as part of automated URL detonation
  • Threat-intel enrichment tooling (TIP, VirusTotal relay) performing passive DNS lookups of the IOC
  • DNS sinkhole or protective-DNS solution logging the query as it blocks it (still worth confirming which host asked)
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: identify the host/user that initiated the connection (InitiatingProcessFileName should be a browser); confirm it is not a designated analyst/sandbox host, and check whether the request succeeded (ActionType=ConnectionSuccess vs a blocked/sinkholed response). 2) CONTAIN: isolate the host if a browser reached the live portal, block npmjs.help network-wide, and force password + MFA re-enrollment for the user, revoking any npm/GitHub personal-access tokens they hold. 3) HUNT: correlate back to EmailEvents/UrlClickEvents for npmjs.help to scope all recipients, and audit the org's npm dependency lockfiles/CI logs for installation of the compromised chalk/debug/ansi-styles versions pulled during the exposure window.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ActionType, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK

    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Microsoft SharePoint contains a deserialization of untrusted data vulnerability which could allow an unauthorized attacker to execute code over a network. Vendor: Microsoft, Product: SharePoint. Federal patch due: 2026-07-25.

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-50522")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-50522")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
